<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We give a uniform deterministic quasipolynomial-time algorithm for finite turn-based stochastic mean-payoff games with signed integer rewards and rational chance-transition probabilities encoded in binary. It computes exactly the vertices of nonnegative value, including value zero, for the expectation of the pathwise liminf mean payoff. The algorithm uses exact rational arithmetic and $`2^{O((\log(L+2))^2)}`$ bit operations, where <i>L</i> is the complete binary input length.

- **C1** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): There are a uniform deterministic Turing machine and an absolute constant $C$ such that, on every valid input described above, the machine outputs exactly $U$ in at most $$2^{C(\log_2(L+2))^2}$$ bit operations. The bound includes input reading, exact rational arithmetic, and output. Vertices of value zero are included.
  - Explanation: Computes exactly the vertices of nonnegative value with deterministic quasipolynomial bit cost, including equality at zero.
- **C2** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-discount)): For every game in the input class, there are vectors $G,\psi\in\mathbb R^V$ and a pure positional strategy pair $(\sigma^*,\tau^*)$ whose selected edges attain the extrema defining $f_u(v(u))$ for every $0<u\le1/(2H)$. Moreover, $$v_i(u)=G_i+u\psi_i+O(u^2) \qquad(u\downarrow0). \label{eq:gain-bias-expansion}$$ For every start vertex $i$ and every behavioral opposing strategy, $$\mathbb E_{i,\sigma^*,\tau}[\operatorname{MP}_w]\ge G_i, \qquad \mathbb E_{i,\sigma,\tau^*}[\operatorname{MP}_w]\le G_i. \label{eq:gain-strategy-bounds}$$ Consequently $G_i=\operatorname{val}(i)$. Each $G_i$ is rational with a representation whose denominator has absolute value at most $A_0$, and $$\begin{gathered} \|G_i\|\le W, \qquad G_i\ne0\ \Longrightarrow\ \|G_i\|\ge\frac1H,\\ \|v_i(\lambda_0)-G_i\|\le\frac1{16H}. \end{gathered} \label{eq:discount-gap-error}$$
  - Explanation: A single positional pair works throughout a small-discount interval; its rational limiting gain is the expected pathwise liminf value and is separated from zero when nonzero.
- **C3** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-finite-submartingale)): Let $(Z_t)_{t\ge0}$ be adapted to a filtration $(\mathcal F_t)_{t\ge0}$ and take values in a fixed finite subset of $\mathbb R$. If $\mathbb E[Z_{t+1}\mid\mathcal F_t]\ge Z_t$ for every $t$, then $Z_t$ is eventually constant almost surely. Its eventual value $Z_\infty$ satisfies $\mathbb EZ_\infty\ge\mathbb EZ_0$.
  - Explanation: A finite-valued process with nonnegative conditional drift stabilizes almost surely and preserves the initial expectation inequality.
- **C4** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-bounded-martingale-average)): Let $(\mathcal F_t)_{t\ge0}$ be a filtration. Suppose $D_t$ is $\mathcal F_{t+1}$-measurable, $\mathbb E[D_t\mid\mathcal F_t]=0$, and $\|D_t\|\le c$ for a fixed finite constant $c$. Then $T^{-1}\sum_{t<T}D_t\to0$ almost surely.
  - Explanation: Uniformly bounded centered adapted increments have almost surely vanishing averages.
- **C5** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-labeling)): Let $F:\mathbb R^n\to\mathbb R^n$ satisfy Equation <a href="#eq:label-map" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:label-map">[eq:label-map]</a>. There is a terminating deterministic procedure $\mathcal L(A,D,a,b)$, using exact evaluations of $\lfloor F(t)\rfloor$ for $t\in\mathbb Z^n$ and integer and rational arithmetic, with the following guarantee. For every input as in Equation <a href="#eq:label-width" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:label-width">[eq:label-width]</a> and Definition <a href="#def:label-witness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="def:label-witness">5</a>, its output $\ell\in\{+,-\}^n$ satisfies $$\begin{aligned} y_i\ge A_i+D-P&\ \Longrightarrow\ \ell_i=+ &&\text{for every plus witness }y,\\ z_i\le A_i+P&\ \Longrightarrow\ \ell_i=- &&\text{for every minus witness }z. \end{aligned}$$
  - Explanation: Exact recursive labeling simultaneously respects every mass-admissible boundary witness without computing the witnesses.
- **C6** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-label-comparison)): Every subsolution $y$ and supersolution $z$ in the same box satisfy $y\le z$. Starting at $t^0=u$, the iteration $$t^{m+1}=T(t^m),\qquad T(t)=\min\{u,\max\{A,\lfloor F(t)\rfloor\}\}, \label{eq:label-floor}$$ stops at a vector $t^*$ after at most $nD+1$ evaluations of $T$. For every such pair $y,z$ one has $y\le t^*\le z$.
  - Explanation: Clipped floor iteration terminates in the integer box and sandwiches every subsolution and supersolution.
- **C7** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-label-mechanics)): Every child input is valid and its box lies inside its parent’s box. If the child calls terminate, each pivot pass makes at most $1024n+1$ calls. The width base case makes at most $522240n+1$ evaluations of $\lfloor F\rfloor$.
  - Explanation: Centered child boxes are valid and contained in the parent; the pivot passes and width base case have explicit finite evaluation bounds.
- **C8** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-label-rank)): The procedure terminates. At a nonbase call every child has smaller rank $d+B(\mu(a,b))$. There is exactly one child preserving $B$, the provisional call, and it decreases $d$ by one. There are at most $2048n+3$ other children; each decreases $B$ by at least one and does not increase $d$.
  - Explanation: A rank combining width exponent and mass-product budget decreases on every recursive edge, with only the provisional child preserving the budget.
- **C9** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-label-translation)): Let $r\in\{q,D/2\}$ and let $y$ or $z$ be a relevant witness of the parent box. Define, according to its sign, $$\begin{aligned} y'_i&=\max\{k_i-r/2,\ y_i-s_y(k)+r/2\},\\ z'_i&=\min\{k_i+r/2,\ z_i+s_z(k)-r/2\}. \end{aligned} \label{eq:label-translation}$$ The resulting vector is an integer subsolution or supersolution, respectively, in the centered width-$r$ box. Its support is exactly $\{i:g_i(k)<r\}$, which is contained in the parent’s support. It claims every index with $g_i(k)\le r/32$. It is a witness for that child whenever its support has mass at most one for the child’s mass vector of the corresponding sign.
  - Explanation: Translation in the appropriate direction and clipping preserve witness inequalities; the surviving support determines admissibility under child masses.
- **C10** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-label-down)): For every relevant plus witness $y$, the peak $s_y(k)$ is constant throughout the downward pass and at most $D/2$. No plus witness claims an index in $I_-$. Every minus witness $z$ with a claim outside $I_-$ satisfies $$b\bigl(\{i:g_i^z(k^{\downarrow})<q\}\bigr)>3/4. \label{eq:label-down-mass}$$
  - Explanation: Downward movement preserves plus peaks and excludes plus claims from minus overrides while forcing concentrated minus mass at stopping.
- **C11** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-label-up)): For every relevant minus witness $z$, the peak $s_z(k)$ is constant throughout the upward pass and at most $D/2$. No minus witness claims an index in $I_+$. At the final pivot the following mass inequalities hold: $$\begin{aligned} b\bigl(\{i:g_i^z(k^f)<q\}\bigr)&>3/4 &&\text{if $z$ has a claim outside $I_-$}, \label{eq:label-final-minus-mass}\\ a\bigl(\{i:g_i^y(k^f)<q\}\bigr)&>3/4 &&\text{if $y$ has a claim outside $I_+$}. \label{eq:label-final-plus-mass} \end{aligned}$$
  - Explanation: Upward movement preserves minus peaks and the earlier minus mass bound, excludes minus claims from plus overrides, and forces concentrated plus mass.
- **C12** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-comparison-vectors)): The vectors in <a href="#eq:comparison-vectors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:comparison-vectors">[eq:comparison-vectors]</a> satisfy $$F(Y)\ge Y,\qquad F(Z)\le Z,\qquad Y\le x\le Z, \qquad \\|Y\\|_\infty,\\|Z\\|_\infty\le J.$$ For every coordinate $i$, $$Z_i-Y_i\le 2(N+1)=K/128. \label{eq:comparison-gap}$$
  - Explanation: Integer comparison vectors flank the scaled fixed point, satisfy opposite inequalities, and have uniformly bounded separation.
- **C13** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-box-invariant)): Every box in this iteration contains $Y$ and $Z$. The iteration makes at most $128(d_0+1)$ calls to $\mathcal L$ and ends with $D\le K$.
  - Explanation: Every shrinking box retains both comparison vectors and eventually reaches the terminal width.

The vertex set is partitioned as $$V=V_{\\max}\\sqcup V_{\\min}\\sqcup V_{\\mathrm{ch}}.$$ Each edge $e$ has an integer reward $w(e)$. At a vertex of $V_{\\max}$, Max chooses the next edge; at a vertex of $V_{\\min}$, Min chooses it; at a vertex $i\\in V_{\\mathrm{ch}}$, the next edge is drawn from a prescribed rational probability distribution $p_i$ on the outgoing edges. Rewards and the numerators and positive denominators of the probabilities are encoded in binary. The graph and all edge data are listed explicitly. Loops, parallel edges, zero probabilities, and unreduced fractions are permitted.",
```json
          "source_id": "canonical:PM-104.1"
        }
      ],
      "id": "C2",
      "kind": "theorem",
      "quantifiers": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-thm-discount",
          "exact_quote": "For every game in the input class, there are vectors $G,\\psi\\in\\mathbb R^V$ and a pure positional strategy pair $(\\sigma^*,\\tau^*)$ whose selected edges attain the extrema defining $f_u(v(u))$ for every $0<u\\le1/(2H)$. Moreover, $$v_i(u)=G_i+u\\psi_i+O(u^2)\n                 \\qquad(u\\downarrow0).\n\\label{eq:gain-bias-expansion}$$ For every start vertex $i$ and every behavioral opposing strategy, $$\\mathbb E_{i,\\sigma^*,\\tau}[\\operatorname{MP}_w]\\ge G_i,\n \\qquad\n \\mathbb E_{i,\\sigma,\\tau^*}[\\operatorname{MP}_w]\\le G_i.\n\\label{eq:gain-strategy-bounds}$$ Consequently $G_i=\\operatorname{val}(i)$. Each $G_i$ is rational with a representation whose denominator has absolute value at most $A_0$, and $$\\begin{gathered}\n |G_i|\\le W,\n \\qquad G_i\\ne0\\ \\Longrightarrow\\ |G_i|\\ge\\frac1H,\\\\\n |v_i(\\lambda_0)-G_i|\\le\\frac1{16H}.\n \\end{gathered}\n\\label{eq:discount-gap-error}$$",
          "source_id": "canonical:PM-104.1"
        }
      ],
      "statement_anchor": "stmt-thm-discount"
    },

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-finite-submartingale",
        "exact_quote": "Let $(Z_t)_{t\\ge0}$ be adapted to a filtration $(\\mathcal F_t)_{t\\ge0}$ and take values in a fixed finite subset of $\\mathbb R$.       },
```

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **Input game** ([source](#pm-h-3)): An instance consists of a finite directed multigraph $G=(V,E)$ with $V=\{1,\ldots,n\}$, $n\ge1$, and at least one outgoing edge at every vertex. The vertex set is partitioned as $$V=V_{\max}\sqcup V_{\min}\sqcup V_{\mathrm{ch}}.$$ Each edge $e$ has an integer reward $w(e)$. At a vertex of $V_{\max}$, Max chooses the next edge; at a vertex of $V_{\min}$, Min chooses it; at a vertex $i\in V_{\mathrm{ch}}$, the next edge is drawn from a prescribed rational probability distribution $p_i$ on the outgoing edges. Rewards and the numerators and positive denominators of the probabilities are encoded in binary. The graph and all edge data are listed explicitly. Loops, parallel edges, zero probabilities, and unreduced fractions are permitted.
- **Pure positional strategy** ([source](#pm-h-3)): A pure positional strategy chooses one outgoing edge at each vertex of its player, independently of the preceding history.
- **Pathwise mean payoff and game value** ([source](#pm-h-3)): For a play $\pi=(X_0,e_0,X_1,e_1,\ldots)$ define $$\operatorname{MP}_w(\pi)=\liminf_{T\to\infty}\frac1T\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}w(e_t), \qquad \operatorname{val}(i)=\sup_\sigma\inf_\tau \mathbb E_{i,\sigma,\tau}[\operatorname{MP}_w].$$
- **Nonnegative-value set** ([source](#pm-h-3)): If $L$ is the complete binary input length, the decision problem is to output the set $$U=\{i\in V:\operatorname{val}(i)\ge0\}.$$
- **Discounted map** ([source](#eq:discount-edge)): For $0<u<1$, define $f_u:\mathbb R^V\to\mathbb R^V$ by taking at vertex $i$ the maximum, minimum, or prescribed chance average of $$u w(e)+(1-u)t_j, \qquad e:i\longrightarrow j, \label{eq:discount-edge}$$ according as $i$ belongs to Max, Min, or Chance.
- **Labeling input** ([source](#pm-h-11)): An input consists of $A\in\mathbb Z^n$, a width $$D=h2^d,\qquad 128\le h\le255,\quad h,d\in\mathbb Z,\quad d\ge0, \label{eq:label-width}$$ and positive rational mass vectors $a,b\in\mathbb Q_{>0}^n$.
- **Subsolutions, supersolutions, and signed witnesses** ([source](#stmt-def-label-witness)): An integer vector $y\in[A,u]$ is a *subsolution in the box* if $$F_i(y)\ge y_i\quad\text{for every }i\in S_y, \qquad S_y=\{i:y_i>A_i\}.$$ It is a *plus witness* if also $a(S_y)\le1$; its claims are the indices $i$ for which $y_i\ge u_i-P$. An integer vector $z\in[A,u]$ is a *supersolution in the box* if $$F_i(z)\le z_i\quad\text{for every }i\in S_z, \qquad S_z=\{i:z_i<u_i\}.$$ It is a *minus witness* if also $b(S_z)\le1$; its claims are the indices $i$ for which $z_i\le A_i+P$. The sets $S_y,S_z$ are the respective supports. A witness is *relevant* if it has at least one claim.
- **Centered call** ([source](#pm-h-12)): A *centered call* of even width $r$ at an integer pivot $k$ means the call $\mathcal L(k-(r/2)\mathbf 1,r,a',b')$ with the indicated masses.
- **Comparison vectors** ([source](#eq:comparison-vectors)): Define the integer vectors $$Y=\lfloor x\rfloor-N\mathbf 1, \qquad Z=\lceil x\rceil+N\mathbf 1. \label{eq:comparison-vectors}$$

| id | quantity | relation | value | regime |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Q1 | Overall bit-operation upper bound | ≤ | $2^{C(\log_2(L+2))^2}$ | Complete binary input length; absolute constant unspecified. |
| Q2 | W | = | $\max\bigl(1,\max_{e\in E}\|w(e)\|\bigr)$ | Supplied unreduced positive denominators; empty product is one. |
| Q3 | Q | = | $\prod_{\substack{e\text{ outgoing from}\\i\in V_{\mathrm{ch}}}} \operatorname{den}(p_i(e))$ | Supplied unreduced positive denominators; empty product is one. |
| Q4 | A_0 | = | $n!\,[Q(3+W)]^n$ | Exact definitions from the encoded game. |
| Q5 | H | = | $(W+4)A_0$ | Exact definitions from the encoded game. |
| Q6 | Discount | = | $\frac1{32H^2}$ | Exact definitions from the encoded game. |
| Q7 | Nonzero gain magnitude | ≥ | $\frac1H$ | Every start vertex; nonzero condition applies to the gain gap. |
| Q8 | Discount approximation error | ≤ | $\frac1{16H}$ | Every start vertex; nonzero condition applies to the gain gap. |
| Q9 | N | = | $32H^2$ | Exact integer parameter definitions. |
| Q10 | K | = | $256(N+1)$ | Exact integer parameter definitions. |
| Q11 | S | = | $16HK$ | Exact integer parameter definitions. |
| Q12 | J | = | $SW+N+1$ | Exact integer parameter definitions. |
| Q13 | Comparison-vector separation | ≤ | $2(N+1)$ | Every coordinate. |
| Q14 | Fixed-point approximation error | ≤ | $\frac1{16H}$ | At termination with width at most the terminal threshold. |
| Q15 | Game-value approximation error | ≤ | $\frac1{8H}$ | Every vertex at termination. |
| Q16 | Normalized output threshold | = | $-\frac1{2H}$ | Threshold on the normalized lower endpoint, equivalent to the integer output test. |
| Q17 | Pivot step size | = | $\frac D{4096}$ | Nonbase calls. |
| Q18 | Mass-product growth | = | $\frac{32}{25}$ | Continuation child; pivot children grow at least as much. |
| Q19 | Calls per pivot pass | ≤ | $1024n+1$ | Child calls terminate. |
| Q20 | Width-base floor evaluations | ≤ | $522240n+1$ | Width base case. |
| Q21 | Budget-decreasing children | ≤ | $2048n+3$ | Each nonbase call. |
| Q22 | Outer labeling calls | ≤ | $128(d_0+1)$ | Shrinking from the initial permitted width. |
| Q23 | Recursive tree node bound | ≤ | $2^{O((\log_2(L+2))^2)}$ | Uniform initial masses and polynomial initial width exponent. |

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **R1** [claim](#stmt-thm-discount) / [proof](#pm-h-7): Bound integer determinant coefficients and stabilize all edge comparison signs near zero. -> A positional pair valid for every sufficiently small discount.
- **R2** [claim](#stmt-thm-discount) / [proof](#pm-h-8): Cancel the lowest determinant power and bound the rational gain and discount error. -> Rational gains, a nonzero-value gap, and a discount approximation bound.
- **R3** [claim](#stmt-thm-discount) / [proof](#pm-h-9): Apply finite-valued drift stabilization and vanishing bounded martingale averages to telescope bias residuals. -> Gain equals the expected pathwise liminf game value.
- **R4** [claim](#stmt-thm-labeling) / [proof](#pm-h-12): Run the base cases, two capped pivot passes, provisional labels, mass reweighting, and continuation overrides. -> A terminating recursive label vector.
- **R5** [claim](#stmt-thm-labeling) / [proof](#pm-h-13): Translate witnesses in opposite directions and clip into centered boxes. -> Child witness inequalities and exact surviving supports.
- **R6** [claim](#stmt-thm-labeling) / [proof](#pm-h-15): Use concentrated matching mass to retain witnesses after reweighting and apply safe overrides. -> Simultaneous correctness for every signed witness claim.
- **R7** [claim](#stmt-lem-comparison-vectors) / [proof](#pm-h-17): Scale the discounted operator and place rounded integer comparison vectors on opposite sides of its fixed point. -> Nearby subsolution and supersolution used only for proof.
- **R8** [claim](#stmt-lem-box-invariant) / [proof](#pm-h-18): Use contrapositive boundary labels and the permitted next width to shrink while retaining the comparison vectors. -> Terminal box giving the required approximation.
- **R9** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#subsec:bit-complexity): Count paths by budget-decreasing edges and bound exact local arithmetic and stack storage. -> Uniform deterministic quasipolynomial bit bound.

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **I-order**: The stopped vector lies above every integer subsolution and below every integer supersolution in the same box.
- **I-stop**: Starting at the upper face, the clipped-floor iteration stops after at most $nD+1$ evaluations.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **A-numerical**: Replace exact floors by floating-point rounding. -> A rounding error changes the integer box update and can invalidate the witness bounds.
- **A-runtime**: Use the finite box iteration alone as the quasipolynomial game algorithm. -> Its bound depends on the numerical width; binary input can describe a very large box.
- **A-objective**: Apply the decision rule to an unbounded process or a different payoff convention. -> The finite input and expected pathwise lower-average guarantees do not establish the new objective.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `algorithmic`. The finite box iteration is constructive and uses exact arithmetic. This executable component computes the stopped vector in the finite comparison lemma; coverage is limited to the admitted discounted rational operators and boxes.

```python
# INPUTS: JSON A, D, gamma, rows; each edge is [target, offset, probability].
# PRECONDITIONS: integer A; 1 <= n <= 2; D = 128; 0 < gamma < 1; nonempty max/min/chance rows; rational data; chance probabilities nonnegative and sum to 1.
# OUTPUTS: JSON greatest integer subsolution of the clipped box, the stopped vector t*.
# TIME: at most n*D+1 map evaluations, each linear in the listed edges with exact rational bit costs.
# SPACE: linear in vertices and listed edges, apart from exact rational operand lengths.
# ARITHMETIC: certified exact fmpq rationals and integer floors; no floating point.
# FAILURE: malformed inputs or violated contracts raise ValueError; exceeding the proven iteration bound raises RuntimeError.
import json, sys
from flint import fmpq

def solve(data):
    A = data['A']
    D = data['D']
    n = len(A)
    gamma = fmpq(data['gamma'])
    rows = data['rows']
    if not (1 <= n <= 2 and type(D) is int and D == 128 and len(rows) == n and 0 < gamma < 1):
        raise ValueError('box or discount')
    if any(type(a) is not int for a in A):
        raise ValueError('noninteger endpoint')
    parsed = []
    for row in rows:
        kind = row['kind']
        edges = [(e[0], fmpq(e[1]), fmpq(e[2])) for e in row['edges']]
        if kind not in ('max', 'min', 'chance') or not edges:
            raise ValueError('row')
        if any(type(j) is not int or not 0 <= j < n or p < 0 for j, b, p in edges):
            raise ValueError('edge')
        if kind == 'chance' and sum((p for j,b,p in edges), fmpq(0)) != 1:
            raise ValueError('probabilities')
        parsed.append((kind, edges))
    upper = [a + D for a in A]
    t = upper.copy()
    for step in range(n * D + 1):
        nxt = []
        for i, (kind, edges) in enumerate(parsed):
            values = [gamma * t[j] + bias for j, bias, p in edges]
            if kind == 'max':
                value = max(values)
            elif kind == 'min':
                value = min(values)
            else:
                value = sum((p * v for (j,b,p),v in zip(edges,values)), fmpq(0))
            floor_value = int(value.floor())
            nxt.append(min(upper[i], max(A[i], floor_value)))
        if nxt == t:
            return t
        t = nxt
    raise RuntimeError('iteration bound exceeded')

if __name__ == '__main__':
    print(json.dumps(solve(json.load(sys.stdin)), separators=(',', ':')))
```

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **L1** (hypothesis): The algorithm requires a finite, explicitly listed directed multigraph with an outgoing edge at every vertex. Blocked use: ['Direct application to unbounded state spaces or implicit graphs without an established reduction.']
- **L2** (hypothesis): Chance transitions must have prescribed rational probability distributions, and rewards must be integers encoded in binary. Blocked use: ['Replacing specified transition laws by empirical estimates without establishing the required input model.']
- **L3** (formal_scope): The payoff takes expectation after the pathwise lower limit; changing this order requires a separate justification. Blocked use: ['Interchanging expectation and the lower limit as if the theorem established that interchange.']
- **L4** (formal_scope): The computed output is the nonnegative-value vertex set, including vertices of value zero. Blocked use: ['Claiming that the stated output also contains exact numerical values or optimal strategy descriptions.']
- **L5** (edge_case): Loops, parallel edges, zero probabilities, and unreduced fractions are allowed; implementations must preserve their stated semantics. Blocked use: ['Rejecting valid inputs solely for these features or merging parallel edges while discarding their reward contribution.']
- **L6** (hypothesis): The reusable labeling theorem requires monotonicity and the discounted scalar translation identity. Blocked use: ['Applying the labeling procedure to an arbitrary nonlinear operator without proving both required properties.']
- **L7** (approximation): Ordinary contraction iteration has a dependence on the inverse discount that can be exponential in input length; the stated complexity uses recursive comparison. Blocked use: ['Attributing the quasipolynomial guarantee to ordinary contraction iteration alone.']
- **L8** (nonconstructive): The selected positional pair is an existence ingredient of the proof and need not be computed by the decision algorithm. Blocked use: ['Presenting the existence proof as an implemented strategy synthesis routine.']
- **L9** (nonconstructive): The integer comparison vectors are proof objects that the algorithm does not compute. Blocked use: ['Requiring the implementation to know these comparison vectors before shrinking the box.']
- **L10** (hypothesis): The arithmetic and rounding operations are exact and coordinatewise; numerical approximations need a separate error argument. Blocked use: ['Substituting uncertified floating-point comparisons for exact threshold or floor decisions.']
- **L11** (counterexample_scope): The stated reachability reduction concerns the threshold $1/2$ and uses absorbing reward assignments. Blocked use: ['Treating arbitrary workflow metrics or acceptance checks as automatically covered by this reachability reduction.']
- **L12** (formal_scope): The running-time guarantee is measured in the complete binary input length, including exact arithmetic and output. Blocked use: ['Interpreting the bound as polynomial time or as an empirical performance guarantee.']

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We give a uniform deterministic quasipolynomial-time algorithm for finite turn-based stochastic mean-payoff games with signed integer rewards and rational chance-transition probabilities encoded in binary. It computes exactly the vertices of nonnegative value, including value zero, for the expectation of the pathwise liminf mean payoff. The algorithm uses exact rational arithmetic and $`2^{O((\log(L+2))^2)}`$ bit operations, where <i>L</i> is the complete binary input length.

<a id="pm-h-1"></a>

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

Mean-payoff games ask how two players can control the long-run average reward of an infinite play. In the stochastic version, some transitions are chosen by chance. This combines strategic choice with the possibility of moving between regions having different long-run rewards. The input may describe very small transition probabilities and very large rewards with only a few binary digits, so an algorithm whose cost depends on their magnitudes need not be efficient in the input length.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### The problem and the result

An instance consists of a finite directed multigraph $G=(V,E)$ with $V=\{1,\ldots,n\}$, $n\ge1$, and at least one outgoing edge at every vertex. The vertex set is partitioned as $$V=V_{\max}\sqcup V_{\min}\sqcup V_{\mathrm{ch}}.$$ Each edge $e$ has an integer reward $w(e)$. At a vertex of $V_{\max}$, Max chooses the next edge; at a vertex of $V_{\min}$, Min chooses it; at a vertex $i\in V_{\mathrm{ch}}$, the next edge is drawn from a prescribed rational probability distribution $p_i$ on the outgoing edges. Rewards and the numerators and positive denominators of the probabilities are encoded in binary. The graph and all edge data are listed explicitly. Loops, parallel edges, zero probabilities, and unreduced fractions are permitted.

Both players observe the complete finite history, including traversed edges, and may use arbitrary behavioral strategies with private randomness. Chance draws have the prescribed conditional law at the current vertex. A pure positional strategy chooses one outgoing edge at each vertex of its player, independently of the preceding history. For a play $\pi=(X_0,e_0,X_1,e_1,\ldots)$ define $$\operatorname{MP}_w(\pi)=\liminf_{T\to\infty}\frac1T\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}w(e_t),
 \qquad
 \operatorname{val}(i)=\sup_\sigma\inf_\tau
           \mathbb E_{i,\sigma,\tau}[\operatorname{MP}_w].$$ Here $\sigma$ and $\tau$ range over Max’s and Min’s behavioral strategies, respectively. The expectation is taken after the pathwise lower limit. If $L$ is the complete binary input length, the decision problem is to output the set $$U=\{i\in V:\operatorname{val}(i)\ge0\}.$$

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *There are a uniform deterministic Turing machine and an absolute constant $C$ such that, on every valid input described above, the machine outputs exactly $U$ in at most $$2^{C(\log_2(L+2))^2}$$ bit operations. The bound includes input reading, exact rational arithmetic, and output. Vertices of value zero are included.*

</div>

<a id="subsec:history"></a>
#### Historical context

Shapley introduced stochastic games with positive stopping probabilities and characterized their values by a contraction operator (Shapley 1953) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Shapley1953](#ref-Shapley1953)]. Gillette considered the undiscounted, long-run average objective (Gillette 1957) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[Gillette1957](#ref-Gillette1957)]; Liggett and Lippman supplied the classical stationary-strategy theorem for perfect-information time-average games (Liggett and Lippman 1969) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[LiggettLippman1969](#ref-LiggettLippman1969)]. The relation between discounting and long-run optimization is also classical. Blackwell showed in finite dynamic programming that one stationary policy is optimal for every discount factor sufficiently close to one (Blackwell 1962) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[Blackwell1962](#ref-Blackwell1962)]. Rational discounted values provide the corresponding stabilization mechanism for perfect-information stochastic games; see, for example, Gimbert and Zielonka (Gimbert and Zielonka 2010, Theorem 3) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[GimbertZielonka2010](#ref-GimbertZielonka2010)].

The computational problem connects several familiar classes of games. A simple stochastic game is a finite turn-based reachability game in which chance chooses fairly between two successors. Condon placed its threshold problem in $\mathrm{NP}\cap\mathrm{coNP}$ (Condon 1992) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[Condon1992](#ref-Condon1992)], and Ludwig gave a randomized subexponential algorithm (Ludwig 1995) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[Ludwig1995](#ref-Ludwig1995)]. Zwick and Paterson developed pseudopolynomial algorithms for deterministic mean-payoff games and a reduction to simple stochastic games (Zwick and Paterson 1996) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[ZwickPaterson1996](#ref-ZwickPaterson1996)]. Andersson and Miltersen later proved polynomial-time Turing equivalences among computing values and optimal strategies in simple stochastic, stochastic mean-payoff, and stochastic discounted-payoff games, with numerical parameters—including the discount factor—given in binary (Andersson and Miltersen 2009, Theorem 1) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[AnderssonMiltersen2009](#ref-AnderssonMiltersen2009)]. Randomized strategy-improvement algorithms have subexponential expected bounds (Zwick 2026) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[Zwick2026](#ref-Zwick2026)]. A different line obtains pseudopolynomial bounds when the number of random positions is fixed (Boros et al. 2019; Allamigeon et al. 2025) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[BorosEtAl2019](#ref-BorosEtAl2019), [AllamigeonEtAl2025](#ref-AllamigeonEtAl2025)]. Numerical reward and denominator parameters in such bounds distinguish them from a bound in the complete binary input length. Deterministic quasipolynomial algorithms have also been obtained for simple stochastic games of bounded treewidth (Chatterjee et al. 2023) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[ChatterjeeEtAl2023](#ref-ChatterjeeEtAl2023)].

Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> also gives a deterministic quasipolynomial algorithm for deciding whether the reachability value of a simple stochastic game is at least $1/2$. Make the target absorbing, with reward $+1$ on its self-loop, and give every other edge reward $-1$; any other terminal vertex receives a self-loop of reward $-1$. For every strategy pair the pathwise mean payoff is $2\mathbf 1_{\{\text{target is reached}\}}-1$. Its expectation is twice the reachability probability minus one, so taking the strategy extrema proves the reduction. The transformation increases the input length by at most a constant factor.

An explicit small discount is only one part of the present algorithm. Andersson and Miltersen already bounded a sufficient discount using determinant coefficients and polynomial sign stabilization (Andersson and Miltersen 2009, Lemma 1) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[AnderssonMiltersen2009](#ref-AnderssonMiltersen2009)]; Gaubert, Grand-Clément, and Katz give sharper bounds on Blackwell optimality thresholds (Gaubert, Grand-Clément, and Katz 2025) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[GaubertGrandClementKatz2025](#ref-GaubertGrandClementKatz2025)]. Hansen, Miltersen, and Zwick proved that strategy iteration is strongly polynomial when the discount factor is fixed (Hansen, Miltersen, and Zwick 2013) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[HansenMiltersenZwick2013](#ref-HansenMiltersenZwick2013)]. The dependence of their bound on the inverse distance of that factor from one does not yield a polynomial bound in its binary encoding. Here the discount has polynomial bit length, and a recursive comparison algorithm controls the cost of locating its fixed point.

For games without chance vertices, a deterministic quasipolynomial algorithm in the complete binary input length is given in OpenAI (2026, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[OpenAI2026Deterministic](#ref-OpenAI2026Deterministic)]. Its simultaneous labeling construction is the direct predecessor of the method used here. We extend that construction to discounted operators, so that chance transitions enter through the same order and translation properties as player choices.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### The proof and its main ideas

The first part of the proof replaces the mean-payoff problem by one discounted fixed point. For $0<u<1$, the discounted map takes the maximum, minimum, or chance average of $u w(e)+(1-u)t_j$ over edges $e:i\to j$. It is a contraction and has a unique fixed point $v(u)$. An explicit integer $H$, of polynomial binary length, gives a nonzero-value gap of at least $1/H$ and a discount $\lambda$ for which $\|v(\lambda)-\operatorname{val}\|_\infty\le1/(16H)$. Section <a href="#sec:discount" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:discount">2</a> proves these estimates by elementary bounds on the rational functions of a fixed positional pair. Its gain and bias then give pathwise guarantees against every behavioral opponent. This establishes the payoff convention used here directly.

Finding the discounted fixed point by ordinary contraction iteration would take a number of steps depending on $1/\lambda$, which can be exponential in the input length. The second part of the proof treats any monotone map $F:\mathbb R^n\to\mathbb R^n$ satisfying $$F(t+c\mathbf 1)=F(t)+\gamma c\mathbf 1,
             \qquad 0<\gamma<1.$$ In an integer box, a subsolution is an integer vector $y$ satisfying $F_i(y)\ge y_i$ at coordinates above the lower face; a supersolution satisfies the reverse inequality at coordinates below the upper face. A subsolution close to an upper face requires a plus label there, while a supersolution close to a lower face requires a minus label. The procedure assigns each coordinate a positive mass for each sign. Its guarantee holds simultaneously for all comparison vectors whose coordinates away from the opposite face have total mass at most one.

Section <a href="#sec:labeling" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:labeling">3</a> adapts the two-pass pivot and mass-reweighting method of OpenAI (2026, secs. 4–5) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[OpenAI2026Deterministic](#ref-OpenAI2026Deterministic)]. The crucial translations have a fixed sign: subsolutions move downward and supersolutions upward. The discounted translation identity preserves their required inequalities, allowing comparison vectors in a large box to be used in smaller recursive boxes. Two pivot passes then arrange a redistribution of the masses that keeps the required comparisons valid. At each recursive call, at most one child leaves both mass vectors unchanged. Every other child increases each product of a coordinate’s two masses by a fixed factor. Starting from uniform masses, only $O(\log(n+1))$ such choices are possible along a path, even when the coordinate range is exponentially large.

The final part explains why these boundary labels suffice to find the fixed point. After scaling, we place an integer subsolution $Y$ and an integer supersolution $Z$ on opposite sides of it, with $Z-Y$ uniformly small. Neither vector is computed. A label excluding one comparison vector from a boundary strip permits that boundary to move inward while retaining both. Section <a href="#sec:application" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:application">4</a> shrinks the enclosing box in this way, and the value gap converts the final approximation into the exact nonnegative-value set.

The reusable part of the argument is the labeling procedure: its correctness uses monotonicity and the scalar translation identity, and does not inspect the graph or the transition probabilities. The two-sided integer comparison vectors provide the link from that procedure to a discounted game. Quantitative discounting supplies the required accuracy, while the recursion determines the deterministic quasipolynomial running time.

Throughout, vector inequalities, extrema, and integer rounding are coordinatewise, and $\mathbf 1$ denotes the all-ones vector. The proof uses only exact arithmetic.

<a id="sec:discount"></a>
### A quantitative discount reduction

We first reduce the sign of each mean-payoff value to an approximation of one discounted fixed point. The reduction needs two quantitative facts: nonzero values are separated from zero, and a discount of polynomial bit length approximates every value more accurately than this separation. We prove both facts directly, including the identification of the limiting discounted value with the expected pathwise lower average.

The discounted fixed-point method originates in Shapley’s work (Shapley 1953) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[Shapley1953](#ref-Shapley1953)]. Our argument for a single positional pair at all small discounts is a quantitative version of the eventual-optimality principle introduced by Blackwell for finite Markov decision processes (Blackwell 1962) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[Blackwell1962](#ref-Blackwell1962)]; stationary optimality for perfect-information stochastic games with limiting-average payoffs was established by Liggett and Lippman (Liggett and Lippman 1969) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[LiggettLippman1969](#ref-LiggettLippman1969)]. Quantitative thresholds for small-discount optimality are studied further in (Gaubert, Grand-Clément, and Katz 2025) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[GaubertGrandClementKatz2025](#ref-GaubertGrandClementKatz2025)]. The estimates and the payoff convention needed here are proved below.

Let $n=|V|$, and define <a id="eq:discount-parameters"></a>$$\begin{split}
 W&=\max\bigl(1,\max_{e\in E}|w(e)|\bigr),\\
 Q&=\prod_{\substack{e\text{ outgoing from}\\i\in V_{\mathrm{ch}}}}
       \operatorname{den}(p_i(e)),\\
 A_0&=n!\,[Q(3+W)]^n,
 \qquad H=(W+4)A_0,
 \qquad \lambda_0=\frac1{32H^2}.
\end{split}
\label{eq:discount-parameters}$$ Here $W,Q,A_0,H$ are positive integers. The denominator in the product defining $Q$ is the positive denominator supplied in the input; the fractions need not be reduced. An empty product is one. The explicit input gives $n=O(L)$ and $\log Q,\log W=O(L)$, because the denominator and reward bits are included in $L$. Therefore $$\log H=O\!\left(n\bigl(\log(n+1)+\log Q+\log(W+3)\bigr)\right)
        =O((L+2)^2),$$ so $\lambda_0$ has polynomial bit length.

<a id="eq:discount-edge"></a>
<a id="eq:discount-bound"></a>
For $0<u<1$, define $f_u:\mathbb R^V\to\mathbb R^V$ by taking at vertex $i$ the maximum, minimum, or prescribed chance average of $$u w(e)+(1-u)t_j,
                 \qquad e:i\longrightarrow j,
\label{eq:discount-edge}$$ according as $i$ belongs to Max, Min, or Chance. This map is monotone and is a contraction in the maximum norm, with constant $1-u$. Indeed, each expression in <a href="#eq:discount-edge" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:discount-edge">[eq:discount-edge]</a> changes by at most $(1-u)\|t-t'\|_\infty$, and taking an extremum or an average preserves that bound. Its iterates from zero converge, because the successive differences decrease geometrically. Their limit is the unique fixed point, denoted $v(u)$. Since $f_u$ preserves $[-W,W]^V$, $$\|v(u)\|_\infty\le W.
\label{eq:discount-bound}$$

<a id="stmt-thm-discount"></a>
<div id="thm:discount" class="theorem">

<a id="eq:gain-bias-expansion"></a>
<a id="eq:gain-strategy-bounds"></a>
<a id="eq:discount-gap-error"></a>
**Theorem 2** (Quantitative discount reduction). *For every game in the input class, there are vectors $G,\psi\in\mathbb R^V$ and a pure positional strategy pair $(\sigma^*,\tau^*)$ whose selected edges attain the extrema defining $f_u(v(u))$ for every $0<u\le1/(2H)$. Moreover, $$v_i(u)=G_i+u\psi_i+O(u^2)
                 \qquad(u\downarrow0).
\label{eq:gain-bias-expansion}$$ For every start vertex $i$ and every behavioral opposing strategy, $$\mathbb E_{i,\sigma^*,\tau}[\operatorname{MP}_w]\ge G_i,
 \qquad
 \mathbb E_{i,\sigma,\tau^*}[\operatorname{MP}_w]\le G_i.
\label{eq:gain-strategy-bounds}$$ Consequently $G_i=\operatorname{val}(i)$. Each $G_i$ is rational with a representation whose denominator has absolute value at most $A_0$, and $$\begin{gathered}
 |G_i|\le W,
 \qquad G_i\ne0\ \Longrightarrow\ |G_i|\ge\frac1H,\\
 |v_i(\lambda_0)-G_i|\le\frac1{16H}.
 \end{gathered}
\label{eq:discount-gap-error}$$*

</div>

The proof has an algebraic part and a probabilistic part. Rational functions first give a pair that works for every sufficiently small discount, together with its limiting gain $G$ and first-order term $\psi$. We then use these two vectors to control individual plays against arbitrary behavioral opponents.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### A positional pair valid for all small discounts

Temporarily fix a pure positional pair: one outgoing edge at each Max and Min vertex. Let $P$ be its row-stochastic transition matrix and let $r_i$ be its expected one-step reward at $i$. At a chance vertex the transition probabilities of parallel edges are added in $P$, whereas their possibly different rewards are averaged in $r$. The pair’s discounted equations are <a id="eq:pair-discount-equations"></a>$$x(u)=u r+(1-u)P x(u).
\label{eq:pair-discount-equations}$$ The matrix $I-(1-u)P$ is invertible for $0<u<1$: a vector in its kernel would satisfy $\|x\|_\infty\le(1-u)\|x\|_\infty$. The corresponding affine contraction preserves $[-W,W]^V$, so its solution satisfies $\|x(u)\|_\infty\le W$.

<a id="eq:discount-polynomial-bound"></a>
The determinant argument follows the rational-function sign method of Andersson and Miltersen (Andersson and Miltersen 2009, Lemma 1) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[AnderssonMiltersen2009](#ref-AnderssonMiltersen2009)]; we derive all coefficient bounds in the input parameters above. For a polynomial $a$, write $\|a\|_{\mathrm{coef}}$ for the sum of the absolute values of its coefficients. This norm is subadditive and submultiplicative. Every entry of $QP$ and $Qr$ is an integer. Cramer’s rule therefore gives integer polynomials $N_i,D$ with <a id="eq:discount-cramer"></a>$$x_i(u)=\frac{N_i(u)}{D(u)},
 \qquad D(u)=\det\bigl(Q(I-(1-u)P)\bigr).
\label{eq:discount-cramer}$$ Each entry of the matrix defining $D$ has coefficient norm at most $3Q$. The replacing column defining $N_i$ is $Qu r$, whose entries have coefficient norm at most $QW$. Expanding each determinant over its $n!$ permutations gives $$\|D\|_{\mathrm{coef}}\le A_0,
 \qquad \|N_i\|_{\mathrm{coef}}\le A_0.
\label{eq:discount-polynomial-bound}$$

<a id="eq:discount-edge-comparison"></a>
Whether this pair’s chosen edge is optimal at a player vertex $i$ is determined by comparisons with every outgoing edge $e:i\to j$. Each comparison is the sign of $$x_i(u)-u w(e)-(1-u)x_j(u)
   =\frac{N_i(u)-u w(e)D(u)-(1-u)N_j(u)}{D(u)}.
\label{eq:discount-edge-comparison}$$ The numerator has coefficient norm at most $(3+W)A_0\le H$.

To control these signs, let $a$ be any nonzero integer polynomial with $\|a\|_{\mathrm{coef}}\le H$. Factoring its lowest power of $u$ gives $a(u)=u^\ell(c+u q(u))$, where $c$ is a nonzero integer. For $0<u\le1/(2H)$, $$|u q(u)|\le Hu\le\tfrac12<|c|.$$ Thus the sign of $a(u)$ throughout this interval is the sign of its lowest nonzero coefficient. This observation applies to both the numerator and denominator in <a href="#eq:discount-edge-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:discount-edge-comparison">[eq:discount-edge-comparison]</a>; the denominator is nonzero there by invertibility. Every edge comparison therefore has a constant sign or vanishes identically on the interval.

Now choose attaining edges in the maximum and minimum defining $f_{\lambda_0}(v(\lambda_0))$. They give a pure positional pair $(\sigma^*,\tau^*)$, and its linear solution at $\lambda_0$ equals $v(\lambda_0)$. Since $\lambda_0\le1/(2H)$, all edge inequalities that hold at $\lambda_0$ continue to hold for every $0<u\le1/(2H)$. For each such $u$, the pair’s linear solution is consequently a fixed point of $f_u$, hence equals $v(u)$. The pair is needed for the proof; the algorithm need not compute it.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### The rational gain and its approximation error

Use the polynomials of the pair just selected. Let $\ell$ be the lowest nonzero degree of $D$. Boundedness of $N_i(u)/D(u)$ as $u\downarrow0$ implies that $u^\ell$ also divides each $N_i$. Define $\widehat D=D/u^\ell$ and $\widehat N_i=N_i/u^\ell$. Their coefficient norms satisfy <a href="#eq:discount-polynomial-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:discount-polynomial-bound">[eq:discount-polynomial-bound]</a>, and $\widehat D(0)$ is a nonzero integer. The quotient is analytic at zero, so it has the expansion <a href="#eq:gain-bias-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gain-bias-expansion">[eq:gain-bias-expansion]</a>, with <a id="eq:rational-gain"></a>$$G_i=\frac{\widehat N_i(0)}{\widehat D(0)}.
\label{eq:rational-gain}$$ The bound <a href="#eq:discount-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:discount-bound">[eq:discount-bound]</a> gives $|G_i|\le W$. The displayed denominator has magnitude at most $A_0$, and its numerator is an integer. Thus $G_i\ne0$ implies $|G_i|\ge1/A_0\ge1/H$.

The same coefficient bounds also control the approximation error. The polynomial $\widehat N_i-G_i\widehat D$ has constant coefficient zero and coefficient norm at most $(1+W)A_0$. For $0<u\le1/(2H)$, the tail of $\widehat D$ has magnitude at most $A_0u\le1/2$, so $|\widehat D(u)|\ge1/2$. It follows that <a id="eq:uniform-discount-error"></a>$$|v_i(u)-G_i|
   \le 2(1+W)A_0u
   \le 2Hu.
\label{eq:uniform-discount-error}$$ At $u=\lambda_0$, this is the error claimed in <a href="#eq:discount-gap-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:discount-gap-error">[eq:discount-gap-error]</a>.

<a id="eq:selected-gain-bias"></a>
<a id="eq:chance-gain-bias"></a>
<a id="eq:min-gain-bias"></a>
It remains to identify $G_i$ with the mean-payoff value. The gain controls which transitions can persist, and the coefficient $\psi$ then controls the rewards on those transitions. For a selected player edge $e:i\to j$, substitution of <a href="#eq:gain-bias-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gain-bias-expansion">[eq:gain-bias-expansion]</a> into the discounted equation gives $$G_i=G_j,
               \qquad \psi_i=w(e)+\psi_j-G_j.
\label{eq:selected-gain-bias}$$ At a chance vertex, the corresponding identities are $$\begin{split}
 G_i&=\sum_{e:i\to j}p_i(e)G_j,\\
 \psi_i&=\sum_{e:i\to j}p_i(e)\bigl(w(e)+\psi_j-G_j\bigr).
 \end{split}
\label{eq:chance-gain-bias}$$ At a Min vertex, the inequality $v_i(u)\le u w(e)+(1-u)v_j(u)$ for an arbitrary outgoing edge first gives $G_i\le G_j$. If $G_i=G_j$, subtracting this common constant, dividing by $u$, and letting $u\downarrow0$ gives $$\psi_i\le w(e)+\psi_j-G_j.
\label{eq:min-gain-bias}$$ At a Max vertex, the gain inequality and, when the gains agree, the bias inequality both reverse.

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### From one-step inequalities to pathwise payoffs

We use two elementary facts about adapted processes. Fixing a selected strategy will give the gain along a play one-sided conditional drift. The first lemma shows that the gain then becomes constant, so only gain-preserving player moves can persist and the bias inequalities apply. The second lemma shows that the centered contributions at chance vertices have vanishing averages.

<a id="stmt-lem-finite-submartingale"></a>
<div id="lem:finite-submartingale" class="lemma">

**Lemma 3** (Finite-valued submartingales). *Let $(Z_t)_{t\ge0}$ be adapted to a filtration $(\mathcal F_t)_{t\ge0}$ and take values in a fixed finite subset of $\mathbb R$. If $\mathbb E[Z_{t+1}\mid\mathcal F_t]\ge Z_t$ for every $t$, then $Z_t$ is eventually constant almost surely. Its eventual value $Z_\infty$ satisfies $\mathbb EZ_\infty\ge\mathbb EZ_0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-finite-submartingale"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-finite-submartingale</summary>

*Proof.* Add a constant to make $Y_t=Z_t+c$ nonnegative. Conditional expectation and the assumed drift give $$\begin{split}
 \mathbb E[Y_{t+1}^2-Y_t^2]
 &=\mathbb E[(Y_{t+1}-Y_t)^2]
   +2\mathbb E\!\left[Y_t\,\mathbb E[Y_{t+1}-Y_t\mid\mathcal F_t]\right]\\
 &\ge\mathbb E[(Y_{t+1}-Y_t)^2].
 \end{split}$$ Summing and using boundedness shows that $\mathbb E\sum_{t\ge0}(Y_{t+1}-Y_t)^2<\infty$. The sum is therefore finite almost surely. If there is more than one possible value, let $\Delta>0$ be the minimum distance between distinct values. Every change contributes at least $\Delta^2$ to the sum, so only finitely many changes occur. The singleton case is immediate. Finally, $\mathbb EZ_t\ge\mathbb EZ_0$ for all $t$, and bounded convergence gives the claimed expectation inequality. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-bounded-martingale-average"></a>
<div id="lem:bounded-martingale-average" class="lemma">

**Lemma 4** (Averages of bounded martingale differences). *Let $(\mathcal F_t)_{t\ge0}$ be a filtration. Suppose $D_t$ is $\mathcal F_{t+1}$-measurable, $\mathbb E[D_t\mid\mathcal F_t]=0$, and $|D_t|\le c$ for a fixed finite constant $c$. Then $T^{-1}\sum_{t<T}D_t\to0$ almost surely.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-bounded-martingale-average"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-bounded-martingale-average</summary>

*Proof.* For $s<t$, conditioning on $\mathcal F_t$ gives $\mathbb E[D_sD_t]=0$. Hence $\mathbb E[(\sum_{t<T}D_t)^2]\le Tc^2$. Chebyshev’s inequality at $T=j^2$ gives, for every $\varepsilon>0$, $$\mathbb P\!\left(\left|\frac1{j^2}\sum_{t<j^2}D_t\right|>
          \varepsilon\right)
 \le\frac{c^2}{\varepsilon^2j^2}.$$ The sum over $j$ is finite. The probability that one of these events occurs after index $m$ is at most the tail of this sum, which tends to zero. Applying this argument to $\varepsilon=1,1/2,1/3,\ldots$ proves convergence along squares on a single event of probability one. For $j^2\le T<(j+1)^2$, the additional partial sum has magnitude at most $c(2j+1)$. Dividing by $T\ge j^2$ extends convergence to every $T$. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-bounded-martingale-average-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-bounded-martingale-average</summary>

*Completion of the proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:discount" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:discount">2</a>.* Fix a start vertex $i$, fix Max to $\sigma^*$, and let Min use an arbitrary behavioral strategy. Write $X_t$ for the vertex before the edge $e_t$ is traversed, and let $\mathcal F_t$ be the sigma-field generated by the complete observed history through $X_t$. At a chance vertex the conditional law of $e_t$ is the prescribed distribution. At a player vertex it is the distribution induced by that player’s strategy, including any private randomness.

<a id="eq:gain-limit-expectation"></a>
The process $G_{X_t}$ has nonnegative conditional drift. A selected Max edge preserves $G$ by <a href="#eq:selected-gain-bias" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:selected-gain-bias">[eq:selected-gain-bias]</a>; every Min edge weakly increases $G$; and the first identity in <a href="#eq:chance-gain-bias" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:chance-gain-bias">[eq:chance-gain-bias]</a> gives zero drift at a chance vertex. Lemma <a href="#lem:finite-submartingale" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:finite-submartingale">3</a> gives an eventual value $G_\infty$ with $$\mathbb EG_\infty\ge G_i.
\label{eq:gain-limit-expectation}$$

Define the one-step residual and its chance part by $$R_t=w(e_t)+\psi_{X_{t+1}}-\psi_{X_t}-G_{X_t},
 \qquad
 D_t=\mathbf 1_{\{X_t\in V_{\mathrm{ch}}\}}R_t.$$ These random variables are uniformly bounded because the arena is finite. At a chance vertex, the two identities in <a href="#eq:chance-gain-bias" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:chance-gain-bias">[eq:chance-gain-bias]</a> imply $$\mathbb E[R_t\mid\mathcal F_t]
   =\sum_{e:X_t\to j}p_{X_t}(e)(w(e)+\psi_j)
      -\psi_{X_t}-G_{X_t}=0.$$ Outside chance vertices $D_t=0$. Thus $\mathbb E[D_t\mid\mathcal F_t]=0$, and Lemma <a href="#lem:bounded-martingale-average" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:bounded-martingale-average">4</a> gives $T^{-1}\sum_{t<T}D_t\to0$ almost surely.

<a id="eq:gain-bias-telescoping"></a>
After $G_{X_t}$ has become constant, every traversed nonchance edge preserves $G$. Its residual is zero at Max vertices by <a href="#eq:selected-gain-bias" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:selected-gain-bias">[eq:selected-gain-bias]</a>, and nonnegative at Min vertices by <a href="#eq:min-gain-bias" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:min-gain-bias">[eq:min-gain-bias]</a>. The nonchance residuals are therefore nonnegative except for a finite initial segment. Consequently $$\liminf_{T\to\infty}\frac1T\sum_{t<T}R_t\ge0
                    \qquad\text{almost surely}.$$ Telescoping the bias terms gives $$\frac1T\sum_{t<T}w(e_t)
   =\frac1T\sum_{t<T}G_{X_t}
     +\frac1T\sum_{t<T}R_t
     +\frac{\psi_{X_0}-\psi_{X_T}}{T}.
\label{eq:gain-bias-telescoping}$$ The first average tends to $G_\infty$, and the last term tends to zero. Hence $\operatorname{MP}_w\ge G_\infty$ almost surely. Taking expectations and using <a href="#eq:gain-limit-expectation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gain-limit-expectation">[eq:gain-limit-expectation]</a> proves the first inequality in <a href="#eq:gain-strategy-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gain-strategy-bounds">[eq:gain-strategy-bounds]</a>.

For the second inequality, fix Min to $\tau^*$ and let Max be arbitrary. Now $-G_{X_t}$ has nonnegative conditional drift. The same finite-valued lemma yields an eventual gain $G_\infty$ with $\mathbb EG_\infty\le G_i$. The chance residuals still have average zero. At Min vertices the selected edges have residual zero; at Max vertices an edge preserving $G$ has nonpositive residual by the reversed version of <a href="#eq:min-gain-bias" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:min-gain-bias">[eq:min-gain-bias]</a>. Thus the nonchance residuals are eventually nonpositive, and <a href="#eq:gain-bias-telescoping" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gain-bias-telescoping">[eq:gain-bias-telescoping]</a> gives $$\limsup_{T\to\infty}\frac1T\sum_{t<T}w(e_t)
                    \le G_\infty
                    \qquad\text{almost surely}.$$ This also bounds the pathwise lower average. Taking expectations proves the second inequality in <a href="#eq:gain-strategy-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gain-strategy-bounds">[eq:gain-strategy-bounds]</a>.

Max can therefore guarantee $G_i$, and Min can guarantee that the payoff is at most $G_i$, so $\operatorname{val}(i)=G_i$. The payoffs and gain limits in these expectation comparisons are bounded. The argument permits each opposing history-dependent distribution and establishes the expected-liminf convention directly. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:labeling"></a>
### Simultaneous labeling for discounted monotone maps

<a id="eq:label-map"></a>
We now construct the labels that will permit a box to shrink while retaining the integer comparison vectors around the discounted fixed point. The construction depends only on order and on the response to a common translation. We therefore work in the following abstract setting: $n\ge1$ and $F:\mathbb R^n\to\mathbb R^n$ satisfy $$t\le t'\ \Longrightarrow\ F(t)\le F(t'),\qquad
 F(t+c\mathbf 1)=F(t)+\gamma c\mathbf 1
 \quad(t\in\mathbb R^n,\ c\in\mathbb R),\qquad 0<\gamma<1.
 \label{eq:label-map}$$ All vector inequalities, minima, maxima, and floors in this section are coordinatewise. The algorithm uses $F$ only by evaluating $\lfloor F(t)\rfloor$ at integer vectors $t$.

The two pivot passes and subsequent mass change adapt the simultaneous labeling construction of (OpenAI 2026, secs. 4–5) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[OpenAI2026Deterministic](#ref-OpenAI2026Deterministic)]. Here the operator has the discounted translation rule in Equation <a href="#eq:label-map" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:label-map">[eq:label-map]</a>. The proof below establishes the required labeling theorem directly; the signs of the witness translations are what make that adaptation possible.

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### Witnesses and the labeling guarantee

<a id="eq:label-width"></a>
An input consists of $A\in\mathbb Z^n$, a width $$D=h2^d,\qquad 128\le h\le255,\quad h,d\in\mathbb Z,\quad d\ge0,
 \label{eq:label-width}$$ and positive rational mass vectors $a,b\in\mathbb Q_{>0}^n$. Write $u=A+D\mathbf 1$, $P=D/32$, and $a(S)=\sum_{i\in S}a_i$, $b(S)=\sum_{i\in S}b_i$ for $S\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}$. The representation in Equation <a href="#eq:label-width" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:label-width">[eq:label-width]</a> is unique. Its short range of mantissas will later allow small relative decreases in the width.

<a id="stmt-def-label-witness"></a>
<div id="def:label-witness" class="definition">

**Definition 5** (Witnesses and claims). An integer vector $y\in[A,u]$ is a *subsolution in the box* if $$F_i(y)\ge y_i\quad\text{for every }i\in S_y,
 \qquad S_y=\{i:y_i>A_i\}.$$ It is a *plus witness* if also $a(S_y)\le1$; its claims are the indices $i$ for which $y_i\ge u_i-P$.

An integer vector $z\in[A,u]$ is a *supersolution in the box* if $$F_i(z)\le z_i\quad\text{for every }i\in S_z,
 \qquad S_z=\{i:z_i<u_i\}.$$ It is a *minus witness* if also $b(S_z)\le1$; its claims are the indices $i$ for which $z_i\le A_i+P$. The sets $S_y,S_z$ are the respective supports. A witness is *relevant* if it has at least one claim.

</div>

Every claim belongs to its witness’s support, since $0<P<D$. The output must respect all these claims simultaneously, although the algorithm is not given any witness and does not search for one.

<a id="stmt-thm-labeling"></a>
<div id="thm:labeling" class="theorem">

**Theorem 6** (Recursive simultaneous labeling). *Let $F:\mathbb R^n\to\mathbb R^n$ satisfy Equation <a href="#eq:label-map" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:label-map">[eq:label-map]</a>. There is a terminating deterministic procedure $\mathcal L(A,D,a,b)$, using exact evaluations of $\lfloor F(t)\rfloor$ for $t\in\mathbb Z^n$ and integer and rational arithmetic, with the following guarantee. For every input as in Equation <a href="#eq:label-width" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:label-width">[eq:label-width]</a> and Definition <a href="#def:label-witness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="def:label-witness">5</a>, its output $\ell\in\{+,-\}^n$ satisfies $$\begin{aligned}
 y_i\ge A_i+D-P&\ \Longrightarrow\ \ell_i=+
       &&\text{for every plus witness }y,\\
 z_i\le A_i+P&\ \Longrightarrow\ \ell_i=-
       &&\text{for every minus witness }z.
 \end{aligned}$$*

</div>

The existence of a labeling will already follow from the finite box iteration below. The purpose of the recursive construction is to obtain such labels with the controlled branching and decreasing rank of Lemma <a href="#lem:label-rank" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:label-rank">9</a>, replacing dependence on the numerical width by dependence on its exponent.

<a id="stmt-lem-label-comparison"></a>
<div id="lem:label-comparison" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:label-floor"></a>
**Lemma 7** (Comparison and a finite box iteration). *Every subsolution $y$ and supersolution $z$ in the same box satisfy $y\le z$. Starting at $t^0=u$, the iteration $$t^{m+1}=T(t^m),\qquad
 T(t)=\min\{u,\max\{A,\lfloor F(t)\rfloor\}\},
 \label{eq:label-floor}$$ stops at a vector $t^*$ after at most $nD+1$ evaluations of $T$. For every such pair $y,z$ one has $y\le t^*\le z$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-label-comparison"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-label-comparison</summary>

*Proof.* If $m=\max_j(y_j-z_j)>0$, choose $i$ with $y_i-z_i=m$. Since $y_i>z_i\ge A_i$ and $z_i<y_i\le u_i$, both defining inequalities apply at $i$. Monotonicity and the common translation rule give $$m\le F_i(y)-F_i(z)
   \le F_i(z+m\mathbf 1)-F_i(z)=\gamma m,$$ a contradiction.

The map $T$ preserves the finite integer box and is monotone. Moreover $T(u)\le u$, so induction gives $t^{m+1}\le t^m$. Each changing step decreases the nonnegative integer $\sum_i(t_i^m-A_i)$ by at least one. Its initial value is $nD$, which proves the termination bound, including the final unchanged step.

For an integer $y\in[A,u]$, the subsolution condition is equivalent to $y\le T(y)$. Indeed, when $y_i>A_i$, the latter inequality is equivalent to $y_i\le\lfloor F_i(y)\rfloor$, and at the other coordinates it is automatic. If $y$ is a subsolution, induction gives $y\le t^m$: from $y\le t^m$ one obtains $y\le T(y)\le T(t^m)=t^{m+1}$. Thus $y\le t^*$. The fixed point $t^*=T(t^*)$ is itself a subsolution, and the comparison principle gives $t^*\le z$ for every supersolution $z$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### The recursive procedure

A *centered call* of even width $r$ at an integer pivot $k$ means the call $\mathcal L(k-(r/2)\mathbf 1,r,a',b')$ with the indicated masses. All calls use the same map $F$. In a nonbase call set <a id="eq:label-scales"></a>$$c=A+\frac D2\mathbf 1,\qquad M=\frac D8,\qquad
 q=\frac D{128},\qquad p=\frac q{32}=\frac D{4096}.
 \label{eq:label-scales}$$ The pivot will stay between $c-M\mathbf 1$ and $c+M\mathbf 1$. The two passes use narrow centered boxes to identify some labels directly and arrange the remaining witnesses so that the later mass change preserves their admissibility. The downward pass preserves the plus masses, and the upward pass preserves the minus masses. A final wider centered call supplies the signs used to change both mass vectors.

**Procedure $\mathcal L(A,D,a,b)$.** Execute the first applicable base case. If neither applies, execute Steps 3–6 in order.

1.  **Mass base case.** If $a_i b_i>1$ for every $i$, return $+$ at $i$ when $b_i>1$, and return $-$ otherwise.

2.  **Width base case.** If $d<12$, compute $t^*$ by Equation <a href="#eq:label-floor" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:label-floor">[eq:label-floor]</a>. Return $+$ at $i$ when $t_i^*>A_i+D/2$, and return $-$ otherwise.

3.  **Downward pass.** Initialize $k=c$. Make a centered width-$q$ call at $k$ with masses $(a,\frac43 b)$, and use its labels to update all coordinates simultaneously by <a id="eq:label-down-update"></a>$$k_i^{\mathrm{new}}=
     \begin{cases}
      \max\{c_i-M,k_i-p\},&\text{if the child labels }i\text{ by }-,\\
      k_i,&\text{if the child labels }i\text{ by }+.
     \end{cases}
     \label{eq:label-down-update}$$ Replace $k$ by $k^{\mathrm{new}}$ and repeat until an update leaves the pivot unchanged. Denote this last pivot by $k^{\downarrow}$ and set <a id="eq:label-minus-override"></a>$$I_- =\{i:k_i^{\downarrow}<c_i-P\}.
     \label{eq:label-minus-override}$$

4.  **Upward pass.** Start at $k=k^{\downarrow}$. Make a centered width-$q$ call at $k$ with masses $(\frac43 a,b)$, and update simultaneously by <a id="eq:label-up-update"></a>$$k_i^{\mathrm{new}}=
     \begin{cases}
      \min\{c_i+M,k_i+p\},&\text{if the child labels }i\text{ by }+,\\
      k_i,&\text{if the child labels }i\text{ by }-.
     \end{cases}
     \label{eq:label-up-update}$$ Replace $k$ by $k^{\mathrm{new}}$ and repeat until an unchanged update. Denote the last pivot by $k^f$ and set <a id="eq:label-plus-override"></a>$$I_+ =\{i:k_i^f>c_i+P\}.
     \label{eq:label-plus-override}$$

5.  **Provisional labels and new masses.** Make a centered width-$D/2$ call at $k^f$ with masses $(a,b)$. For its output $\ell^0$, define <a id="eq:label-reweight"></a>$$(\bar a_i,\bar b_i)=
     \begin{cases}
      (\frac45 a_i,\frac85 b_i),&\ell_i^0=+,\\[1mm]
      (\frac85 a_i,\frac45 b_i),&\ell_i^0=-.
     \end{cases}
     \label{eq:label-reweight}$$

6.  **Continuation and overrides.** Call $\mathcal L(A,D,\bar a,\bar b)$. In its output replace the signs on $I_-$ by $-$ and those on $I_+\setminus I_-$ by $+$. Return this label vector.

Each pass includes the final child call whose update changes nothing. That call will certify a mass inequality at the stopped pivot. All coordinates in one update use the same old pivot and the same child output; there is no order-dependent coordinate update.

The purpose of the passes is the following mass property. For every witness with a claim outside its own override set—$I_+$ for a plus witness and $I_-$ for a minus witness—the coordinates in its support whose provisional labels match its sign will have total mass greater than $3/4$. Here mass means $a$-mass for a plus witness and $b$-mass for a minus witness. Its original total mass is at most one, so Step 5 will leave its new total mass below $$\frac85-\frac45\cdot\frac34=1.$$ It will therefore remain a witness for the continuation in the unchanged box. At the same time, every product $a_i b_i$ is multiplied by $32/25$. We first use this product growth to prove termination; the subsequent witness analysis proves the stated mass property and hence correctness.

<a id="stmt-lem-label-mechanics"></a>
<div id="lem:label-mechanics" class="lemma">

**Lemma 8** (Valid boxes and bounded passes). *Every child input is valid and its box lies inside its parent’s box. If the child calls terminate, each pivot pass makes at most $1024n+1$ calls. The width base case makes at most $522240n+1$ evaluations of $\lfloor F\rfloor$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-label-mechanics"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-label-mechanics</summary>

*Proof.* In a nonbase call $d\ge12$, so $c,M,q,p$ and the half-widths $q/2,D/4$ are integral. The centered widths have the allowed representations $q=h2^{d-7}$ and $D/2=h2^{d-1}$. The pivot caps keep $c-M\mathbf 1\le k\le c+M\mathbf 1$, and a centered box has width $r\le D/2$. Its boundaries satisfy $$k-\frac r2\mathbf 1\ge A+\frac D8\mathbf 1\ge A,
 \qquad
 k+\frac r2\mathbf 1\le A+\frac{7D}8\mathbf 1\le u.$$ The continuation uses the same box. All masses remain positive rationals.

Every pivot coordinate lies on the grid of spacing $p$ through $c_i$, because $M/p=512$ is integral. In a changing update some coordinate moves by $p$; each coordinate is monotone during a pass and travels at most $2M$, hence moves at most $1024$ times. There are at most $1024n$ changing updates, followed by one unchanged update. Finally, $d<12$ implies $D\le255\cdot2^{11}=522240$, so the width bound follows from Lemma <a href="#lem:label-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:label-comparison">7</a>. ◻

</details>

The continuation preserves the width, so width reduction alone does not prove termination. The mass products provide the missing measure. Define <a id="eq:label-budget"></a>$$\mu(a,b)=\min_i a_i b_i,\qquad \rho=\frac{32}{25},\qquad
 B(\mu)=\min\{j\in\mathbb Z_{\ge0}:\rho^j\mu>1\}.
 \label{eq:label-budget}$$ This integer is used only in the proof, not computed by the procedure.

<a id="stmt-lem-label-rank"></a>
<div id="lem:label-rank" class="lemma">

**Lemma 9** (Rank and branching). *The procedure terminates. At a nonbase call every child has smaller rank $d+B(\mu(a,b))$. There is exactly one child preserving $B$, the provisional call, and it decreases $d$ by one. There are at most $2048n+3$ other children; each decreases $B$ by at least one and does not increase $d$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-label-rank"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-label-rank</summary>

*Proof.* At a nonbase call $B(\mu)>0$, since otherwise the mass base case would apply. Whenever $\mu'\ge\rho\mu$, the definition gives $B(\mu')\le B(\mu)-1$. Both pivot passes multiply every mass product by $4/3\ge\rho$. The continuation multiplies every mass product by $(4/5)(8/5)=\rho$, independently of the provisional label. These children reduce $B$ and preserve or reduce $d$. Only the provisional call keeps the masses; it halves the width and reduces $d$ by one. This proves the rank assertion.

Induction on the nonnegative rank proves termination. The base cases are finite by their definitions and Lemma <a href="#lem:label-mechanics" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:label-mechanics">8</a>. At a nonbase call every child terminates by induction, and the bound on each pivot pass then applies. The number of children decreasing $B$ is at most $2(1024n+1)+1=2048n+3$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### Translating a witness into a centered box

To analyze the labels, we follow an arbitrary parent witness through the centered calls. Translation places its largest deviation from the pivot at one boundary of the child box; clipping removes coordinates far below that largest deviation. The surviving support will identify the mass forced near the largest deviation when a pivot pass stops.

Fix a nonbase input and a pivot $k\in[c-M\mathbf 1,c+M\mathbf 1]$. For a relevant plus witness $y$, define its *peak* and *gaps* by <a id="eq:label-plus-gaps"></a>$$s_y(k)=\max_i(y_i-k_i),\qquad
 g_i^y(k)=s_y(k)-(y_i-k_i).
 \label{eq:label-plus-gaps}$$ For a relevant minus witness $z$, put <a id="eq:label-minus-gaps"></a>$$s_z(k)=\max_i(k_i-z_i),\qquad
 g_i^z(k)=s_z(k)-(k_i-z_i).
 \label{eq:label-minus-gaps}$$ The gaps are nonnegative integers and at least one gap is zero. The following lemma handles both signs, including the inequalities introduced by the discount factor.

<a id="stmt-lem-label-translation"></a>
<div id="lem:label-translation" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:label-translation"></a>
**Lemma 10** (Translation and clipping). *Let $r\in\{q,D/2\}$ and let $y$ or $z$ be a relevant witness of the parent box. Define, according to its sign, $$\begin{aligned}
 y'_i&=\max\{k_i-r/2,\ y_i-s_y(k)+r/2\},\\
 z'_i&=\min\{k_i+r/2,\ z_i+s_z(k)-r/2\}.
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:label-translation}$$ The resulting vector is an integer subsolution or supersolution, respectively, in the centered width-$r$ box. Its support is exactly $\{i:g_i(k)<r\}$, which is contained in the parent’s support. It claims every index with $g_i(k)\le r/32$. It is a witness for that child whenever its support has mass at most one for the child’s mass vector of the corresponding sign.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-label-translation"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-label-translation</summary>

<a id="eq:label-peak-lower"></a>
*Proof.* A parent claim gives, in either case, $$s(k)\ge D/2-M-P=11D/32.
 \label{eq:label-peak-lower}$$ Outside the parent support one has $y_i=A_i$ or $z_i=u_i$. The corresponding deviation is at most $-D/2+M=-3D/8$, so its gap is at least $23D/32>D/2$. Such an index cannot belong to $\{i:g_i(k)<r\}$.

Before clipping, the two expressions on the right of Equation <a href="#eq:label-translation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:label-translation">[eq:label-translation]</a> are $$k_i+r/2-g_i^y(k),\qquad
 k_i-r/2+g_i^z(k),$$ respectively. They lie on the correct side of the far boundary; clipping at the other boundary puts the vector in the child box. These expressions also show directly that the support is exactly the set of gaps strictly below $r$, and that every gap at most $r/32$ gives a claim.

For the plus case let $t=-s_y(k)+r/2<0$, where the strict sign follows from Equation <a href="#eq:label-peak-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:label-peak-lower">[eq:label-peak-lower]</a> and $r/2\le D/4$. One has $y'\ge y+t\mathbf 1$. At an index in the child support, equality holds in that coordinate, and the parent subsolution inequality applies. Therefore $$F_i(y')\ge F_i(y+t\mathbf 1)=F_i(y)+\gamma t
          \ge y_i+t=y'_i,$$ using $\gamma t\ge t$. For the minus case let $t=s_z(k)-r/2>0$. Now $z'\le z+t\mathbf 1$, with coordinatewise equality on the child support, and $$F_i(z')\le F_i(z+t\mathbf 1)=F_i(z)+\gamma t
          \le z_i+t=z'_i,$$ using $\gamma t\le t$. Thus the required inequalities survive both translation and clipping. The stated mass condition is exactly the remaining condition for being a child witness. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
#### What the two pivot passes enforce

We prove correctness by induction on the rank from Lemma <a href="#lem:label-rank" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:label-rank">9</a>. For the remainder of this subsection, fix a nonbase call and assume that all its children satisfy Theorem <a href="#thm:labeling" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:labeling">6</a>. Witnesses, supports, and claims refer to the parent input unless stated otherwise.

In each pass, the sign whose masses are unchanged protects its own peak: coordinates close enough to that peak must receive its sign and remain stationary. The opposite sign has its masses multiplied by $4/3$. At the final unchanged update, any witness of that sign with a claim outside its override set must consequently have mass greater than $3/4$ near its peak. The next two lemmas make these statements precise.

<a id="stmt-lem-label-down"></a>
<div id="lem:label-down" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:label-down-mass"></a>
**Lemma 11** (The downward pass). *For every relevant plus witness $y$, the peak $s_y(k)$ is constant throughout the downward pass and at most $D/2$. No plus witness claims an index in $I_-$. Every minus witness $z$ with a claim outside $I_-$ satisfies $$b\bigl(\{i:g_i^z(k^{\downarrow})<q\}\bigr)>3/4.
 \label{eq:label-down-mass}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-label-down"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-label-down</summary>

*Proof.* Fix a relevant plus witness $y$ and consider an update from $k$. The width-$q$ translation in Lemma <a href="#lem:label-translation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:label-translation">10</a> is a plus witness for the child, since the plus masses are unchanged and its support is contained in $S_y$. Its claims include all indices with $g_i^y(k)\le q/32=p$. The child labels all of them by $+$, so they do not move. In particular, every peak achiever continues to attain the old peak.

At any other coordinate let $\delta_i=k_i-k_i^{\mathrm{new}}$. Then $0\le\delta_i\le p$ and $$y_i-k_i^{\mathrm{new}}
   =s_y(k)-g_i^y(k)+\delta_i<s_y(k).$$ Thus no moving coordinate reaches the old peak, even when several coordinates move simultaneously. The peak is unchanged. At the initial pivot $c$, $y\le u$ gives $s_y(c)\le D/2$. If $y$ claims $i$, it follows that $$k_i^{\downarrow}\ge y_i-D/2
    \ge A_i+D/2-P=c_i-P,$$ so $i\notin I_-$.

For the mass assertion, let $z$ claim $j\notin I_-$. Then $k_j^{\downarrow}\ge c_j-P$ and $z_j\le A_j+P$, whence <a id="eq:label-down-uncapped"></a>$$s_z(k^{\downarrow})\ge D/2-2P=7D/16>D/2-M.
 \label{eq:label-down-uncapped}$$ At a lower-capped coordinate $k_i^{\downarrow}=c_i-M$ one instead has $k_i^{\downarrow}-z_i\le D/2-M$, since $z_i\ge A_i$. Every peak achiever is therefore strictly above the lower cap.

If the mass in Equation <a href="#eq:label-down-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:label-down-mass">[eq:label-down-mass]</a> were at most $3/4$, the width-$q$ translation of $z$ would have support mass at most one under the final child’s minus masses $\frac43 b$. It would be a minus witness and would claim every peak achiever. The child would label such a coordinate by $-$, forcing it to move down because it is above the lower cap. This contradicts the unchanged update. The mass is therefore strictly greater than $3/4$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-label-up"></a>
<div id="lem:label-up" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:label-final-minus-mass"></a>
<a id="eq:label-final-plus-mass"></a>
**Lemma 12** (The upward pass). *For every relevant minus witness $z$, the peak $s_z(k)$ is constant throughout the upward pass and at most $D/2$. No minus witness claims an index in $I_+$. At the final pivot the following mass inequalities hold: $$\begin{aligned}
 b\bigl(\{i:g_i^z(k^f)<q\}\bigr)&>3/4
 &&\text{if $z$ has a claim outside $I_-$},
 \label{eq:label-final-minus-mass}\\
 a\bigl(\{i:g_i^y(k^f)<q\}\bigr)&>3/4
 &&\text{if $y$ has a claim outside $I_+$}.
 \label{eq:label-final-plus-mass}
\end{aligned}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-label-up"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-label-up</summary>

*Proof.* Fix a relevant minus witness $z$. The width-$q$ translation is a minus witness for every child in this pass because the minus masses are unchanged. All coordinates with $g_i^z(k)\le p$ must receive $-$ and stay fixed. In particular a peak achiever stays fixed. At a coordinate with $g_i^z(k)>p$, write $\delta_i=k_i^{\mathrm{new}}-k_i\in[0,p]$. Then $$k_i^{\mathrm{new}}-z_i
    =s_z(k)-g_i^z(k)+\delta_i<s_z(k).$$ No coordinate overtakes the peak, so the peak is constant. At the starting pivot $k^{\downarrow}\le c$ and $z\ge A$, it is at most $D/2$. A minus claim at $i$ consequently implies $$k_i^f\le z_i+D/2\le A_i+P+D/2=c_i+P,$$ excluding $i$ from $I_+$. Because the peak is constant and $k$ only increases during this pass, every gap $g_i^z(k)$ is nonincreasing. Thus the set of gaps below $q$ can only grow, and Equation <a href="#eq:label-down-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:label-down-mass">[eq:label-down-mass]</a> implies Equation <a href="#eq:label-final-minus-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:label-final-minus-mass">[eq:label-final-minus-mass]</a>.

Now let a plus witness $y$ claim $j\notin I_+$. At the final pivot $k_j^f\le c_j+P$ and $y_j\ge A_j+D-P$, so $$s_y(k^f)\ge D/2-2P=7D/16>D/2-M.$$ An upper-capped coordinate $k_i^f=c_i+M$ has $y_i-k_i^f\le D/2-M$, since $y_i\le u_i$. Every peak achiever is strictly below the upper cap. If the mass in Equation <a href="#eq:label-final-plus-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:label-final-plus-mass">[eq:label-final-plus-mass]</a> were at most $3/4$, its width-$q$ translation would be a plus witness for the last child, whose plus masses are $\frac43 a$. Every peak achiever would have label $+$ and would move up, contradicting the final unchanged update. This proves the remaining inequality. ◻

</details>

The two passes have now separated the claims into those that can safely receive an override and those whose witnesses have substantial mass near a peak at $k^f$. The wider provisional call labels all that mass with the witness’s sign. Reducing the masses of that sign then keeps each remaining witness admissible for the continuation. Figure <a href="#fig:labeling-passes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:labeling-passes">1</a> summarizes how the two passes supply the information used in this final step.

<figure id="fig:labeling-passes">

<figcaption>The two passes preserve opposite peaks and force opposite mass inequalities. A remaining witness means one with a claim outside its own override set: $I_{+}$ for plus witnesses and $I_{−}$ for minus witnesses. The upward pass preserves the mass inequality obtained during the downward pass.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-labeling-passes"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:labeling-passes`)

TikZ/PGF source, copied verbatim from the source:

```latex
\begin{tikzpicture}[
  font=\small,
  stage/.style={draw=black!60,rounded corners=2pt,align=left,
    text width=5.7cm,inner sep=8pt},
```
